- 1 - 
Q. 
Please state your name and business address. 
1 
A. 
My name is Yukari Saegusa.  I am the Vice President and 
2 
Treasurer of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, 
3 
Inc. (“Con Edison” or the “Company”).  I am also the 
4 
Treasurer of the Company’s affiliate Orange and Rockland 
5 
Utilities, Inc. (“Orange and Rockland”). My business 
6 
address is 4 Irving Place, New York, New York. 
7 
Q. 
Briefly describe your educational background. 
8 
A. 
I graduated from the University of Pennsylvania, Wharton 
9 
School in 1989 and received a B.S. degree in Economics.  
10 
I received an MBA from the MIT Sloan School of Management 
11 
in 1995. 
12 
Q. 
Please summarize your professional background. 
13 
A. 
I joined Con Edison in March 2013.  Prior to joining Con 
14 
Edison, from 2004 to 2013 I was employed by Barclays as a 
15 
Managing Director in Debt Capital Markets covering the 
16 
United States utility and energy sectors.  I was employed 
17 
from 1995 to 2004 by Citigroup, also in Debt Capital 
18 
Markets covering the United States utility sector.  In my 
19 
roles at Barclays and Citigroup, I was broadly 
20 
responsible for advising utility clients on the design 
21 
and execution of debt, capital-raising, and liability 
22 
management strategies. 
23

- 2 - 
Q. 
Have you previously sponsored testimony before the New 
1 
York State Public Service Commission ("Commission")? 
2 
A. 
Yes. I submitted testimony on behalf of Con Edison in 
3 
Cases 19-E-0065 and 19-G-0066 and on behalf of Orange and 
4 
Rockland in Cases 14-E-0493, 14-G-0494, 18-E-0067, 18-G-
5 
0068, 21-G-0073 and 21-E-0074. 
6 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your direct testimony in these 
7 
proceedings? 
8 
A. 
My direct testimony discusses (1) the current financial 
9 
market environment, (2) the Company’s historic and 
10 
projected capital structure and cost of capital, and (3) 
11 
the Company’s financial challenges and the need to 
12 
maintain access to financial markets at reasonable cost. 
13 
 
14 
I. 
CURRENT FINANCIAL MARKET ENVIRONMENT 
15 
Q. 
Please describe the current state of the financial 
16 
markets. 
17 
A. 
Prior to the beginning of the COVID-19 pandemic, the U.S. 
18 
experienced a historic ten-year period of economic 
19 
expansion.  U.S gross domestic product grew at an annual 
20 
rate of 2.1% in the fourth quarter of 2019 and the 
21 
unemployment rate had dropped from a high of 10.0% in 
22 
October 2009 to 3.6% in December 2019.  Currently, 
23 


--- Page 4 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 3 - 
however, the economy is in the midst of recovering from a 
1 
severe economic retraction and extreme volatility caused 
2 
by the unprecedented and disruptive economic impacts of 
3 
the COVID-19 pandemic. 
4 
Q. 
Can you provide some examples of COVID-19 pandemic 
5 
related economic impacts and volatility?  
6 
A. 
Some of the major economic impacts include U.S. gross 
7 
domestic product falling at an annual rate of 31.4% in 
8 
the second quarter of 2020 before rising again by 33.8% 
9 
in the third quarter of 2020; the unemployment rate 
10 
rising from a low of 3.5% in February 2020 to a peak of 
11 
14.7% in April 2020 before falling back to 4.2% in 
12 
November 2021; and a seasonally adjusted drop in 
13 
industrial production of 12.7% in April 2020 before 
14 
recovering back close to pre-pandemic levels in August 
15 
2021.  
16 
Q. 
How has the COVID-19 pandemic specifically impacted the 
17 
economy of New York State? 
18 
A. 
In order to slow the rate of COVID-19 infections in the 
19 
State, former Governor Cuomo on March 22, 2020 
20 
implemented the “New York State on PAUSE” executive order 
21 
which, among other restrictions on social gatherings, 
22 
ordered all non-essential businesses to shut down until a 
23 


--- Page 5 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 4 - 
series of health monitoring statistics showed sufficient 
1 
improvement.  As a result, according to the State 
2 
Comptroller’s Office, New York State’s job count fell by 
3 
over 1.9 million people in March and April 2020, of which 
4 
only 600,000 had regained employment by September 2020.    
5 
 
Businesses were allowed to slowly reopen through the 
6 
summer and fall of 2020 as health statistics showed 
7 
continuing improvement, but limitations continued 
8 
regarding how many people could gather together in one 
9 
place and under what conditions they could be together.  
10 
Restrictions were briefly lifted in the summer and fall 
11 
of 2021, but concern with new variants prompted Governor 
12 
Kathy Hochul to declare another State of Emergency on 
13 
November 26, 2021.   
14 
Q. 
Has the Company felt any negative economic impacts 
15 
related to the COVID-19 pandemic? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  In March 2020, Con Edison began suspending utility 
17 
service disconnections, certain collection notices, final 
18 
bill collection agency activity, new late payment charges 
19 
and certain other fees for all customers. In June 2020, 
20 
the State of New York enacted the Parker Mosely Act which 
21 
prohibited utilities from disconnecting residential 
22 
customers during the COVID-19 state of emergency.  
23 


--- Page 6 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 5 - 
Subsequent legislation extended protections to certain 
1 
small commercial customers.  As a result, the Company’s 
2 
accounts receivable balance has grown significantly with 
3 
October 2021 residential arrearages over 60 days 
4 
increasing to $768 million and non-residential arrearages 
5 
over 60 days increasing to $408 million compared to 2019 
6 
baselines of approximately $325 million and $85 million, 
7 
respectively. 
8 
Q. 
Has the Company been allowed to recover any of these 
9 
items? 
10 
A. 
Yes, on November 18, 2021 the Commission issued an order 
11 
authorizing a surcharge mechanism for recovery of 
12 
unbilled late payment charges and fees.    
13 
Q. 
Has the pandemic also been disruptive to the capital 
14 
markets? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  The U.S. equity market has experienced much higher 
16 
volatility due to the pandemic as compared to prior 
17 
years.  The VIX index, which measures volatility in the 
18 
equity market, hit an all-time high of 82.7 on March 16, 
19 
2020 and has averaged 26.0 since March 1, 2020 as 
20 
compared to an average of 17.3 over the past ten years.     
21 
Q. 
What has been the impact of the pandemic on the fixed 
22 
income markets? 
23 


--- Page 7 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 6 - 
A. 
Overall, the U.S. fixed income market is now in its third 
1 
decade of a bull market run.  Investors have been willing 
2 
to invest money at record low yields as they look to put 
3 
funds to work in an artificially low interest rate 
4 
environment.  The yield on Moody’s Baa Corporate Bond 
5 
Index recently stood at 3.35% (October 15, 2021) compared 
6 
to a long-term average of 7.09% since January 2, 1986.  
7 
The drive to record low yields began with unprecedented 
8 
actions taken by the U.S. Federal Reserve and central 
9 
banks around the world in response to the 2008 financial 
10 
crisis.  The Federal Reserve and other central banks have 
11 
injected substantial amounts of liquidity into their 
12 
respective economies through multiple rounds of 
13 
quantitative easing.   
14 
Q. 
What do you mean by quantitative easing? 
15 
A. 
Quantitative easing is the practice of using money, newly 
16 
issued by the central banks, to buy mortgage-backed and 
17 
government securities.  The practice increases liquidity 
18 
by injecting money supply into the economy and 
19 
suppressing interest rates by driving the prices of the 
20 
mortgage-backed and government securities up and yields 
21 
on those securities down.      
22 
Q. 
Did the Federal Reserve take action to scale back the 
23 


--- Page 8 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 7 - 
unprecedented actions it took after the 2008 financial 
1 
crisis? 
2 
A. 
 Yes, but only temporarily.  Starting in January 2014, the 
3 
Federal Reserve gradually began to reduce the amount of 
4 
its bond purchases, ending them completely in October 
5 
2014, and signaling an end to its loose monetary policy. 
6 
In the December 2015 meeting of the Federal Open Markets 
7 
Committee (“FOMC”), the Federal Reserve raised the 
8 
Federal Funds rate by 25 basis points (“bps”), further 
9 
signaling the end of an easing cycle and the beginning of 
10 
a hiking cycle.  The Federal Funds rate is the interest 
11 
rate at which a depository institution lends funds 
12 
maintained at the Federal Reserve to another depository 
13 
institution overnight.    The Federal Funds rate is one 
14 
of the most influential interest rates in the U.S. 
15 
economy because it affects monetary and financial 
16 
conditions, which in turn have a bearing on key aspects 
17 
of the economy including employment, growth and 
18 
inflation.  After the December 2015 Federal Funds rate 
19 
increase, the FOMC raised the Federal Funds rate eight 
20 
times (at the December 2016, March 2017, June 2017, 
21 
December 2017, March 2018, June 2018, September 2018 and 
22 
December 2018 meetings) before it reversed course and 
23 


--- Page 9 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 8 - 
lowered rates three times (at the August 2019, September 
1 
2019 and October 2019 meetings).   
2 
Q. 
Has the COVID-19 pandemic caused the Federal Reserve to 
3 
make any further adjustments to the Federal Funds rate? 
4 
A. 
Yes. The onset of the pandemic caused large disruptions 
5 
in liquidity and borrowing rates.  A2/P2 rated commercial 
6 
paper, which had generally been trending with Federal 
7 
Funds rate increases and decreases, saw increases of up 
8 
to 225 basis points between the low and high points of 
9 
March 2020 (see Exhibit_(YS-1)). At the same time, 
10 
longer-term liquidity in the commercial paper market 
11 
shrank with one- to four-day commercial paper spiking up 
12 
to over 85% of total issuance in mid-March from pre-COVID 
13 
levels that varied around 60% (see Exhibit_(YS-2)).  
14 
After the experience of the 2008 financial crisis, the 
15 
Federal Reserve was highly aware that supporting the 
16 
functioning of the funding markets was critical and acted 
17 
quickly to support the commercial paper markets.  Over 
18 
the course of two meetings (March 3, 2020 and March 16, 
19 
2020) the Federal Reserve responded forcefully to 
20 
increase liquidity by cutting the Federal Funds rate by 
21 
an unprecedented 150 basis points to a record low target 
22 
range of 0.00%-0.25% where it remains today. 
23 


--- Page 10 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 9 - 
Q. 
Did these reductions in rates impact the commercial paper 
1 
market? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Since the peak in March, A2/P2 rated commercial 
3 
paper rates have decreased to be in line with the current 
4 
Federal Funds target and the percentage of 1- to 4-day 
5 
commercial paper has decreased back closer to pre-COVID-
6 
19 pandemic levels as longer-term funding became more 
7 
available. 
8 
Q. 
Did the Federal Reserve take any other actions to support 
9 
credit in response to the COVID-19 pandemic?    
10 
A. 
 Yes.  In an effort to limit the economic damage caused by 
11 
the pandemic, the Federal Reserve took other actions to 
12 
increase liquidity and manage interest rates.  Chief 
13 
among them were: 
14 
 
the announcement of $700 billion for a new round of 
15 
quantitative easing (Federal Reserve Press Release, 
16 
March 15, 2020); 
17 
 
Commercial Paper Funding Facility (“CPFF”) to support 
18 
up to $1 trillion of liquidity in the corporate paper 
19 
markets as well as the Primary Dealer Credit Facility 
20 
(“PDCF”), which offers collateralized loans to large 
21 
broker-dealers (Federal Reserve Press Release, March 
22 
17, 2020); 
23 


--- Page 11 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 10 - 
 
Money Market Mutual Fund Liquidity Facility (“MMLF”) 
1 
offering collateralized loans to large banks buying 
2 
assets from money market mutual funds (Federal Reserve 
3 
Press Release, March 18, 2020); and 
4 
 
The establishment of three new emergency lending 
5 
facilities - the Primary Market Corporate Credit 
6 
Facility (“PMCCF”), the Secondary Market Corporate 
7 
Credit Facility (“SMCCF”) and the Term Asset-Backed 
8 
Securities Loan Facility (“TALF”) (Federal Reserve 
9 
Press Release, March 23, 2020). 
10 
 
The U.S. government also stepped in to provide economic 
11 
stimulus by passing the $2.1 trillion Coronavirus Aid, 
12 
Relief, and Economic Security (“CARES”) Act to support 
13 
individuals, businesses, governments and healthcare 
14 
providers impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic, and also the 
15 
Paycheck Protection Program and the Health Care 
16 
Enhancement Act, which provided an additional $484 
17 
billion in emergency aid.  
18 
Q. 
What was the result of intervention from the Federal 
19 
Reserve and U.S. Government? 
20 
A. 
These programs increased liquidity and helped stabilize 
21 
the commercial paper markets by increasing the ability 
22 
for borrowers to access the commercial paper market more 
23 


--- Page 12 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 11 - 
efficiently.  
1 
Q. 
Has the Federal Reserve given projections on how long the 
2 
Federal Funds target will be held at these levels? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  The Federal Reserve publishes a forecast of the 
4 
Federal Funds rate for 2022, 2023, 2024 and longer run.  
5 
The projections are based on the individual assessments 
6 
of the Federal Reserve Board members and Federal Reserve 
7 
Bank presidents.  In the latest forecast (December 2021), 
8 
the median of the FOMC participants’ assessments of 
9 
appropriate monetary policy forecasts the Federal Funds 
10 
rate to increase to 0.9% in 2022, 1.6% in 2023 and 2.1% 
11 
in 2024. 
12 
Q. 
Does the forecasted rise in the Federal Funds rate 
13 
correlate to a rise in short-term borrowing rates? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  As mentioned earlier, changes in commercial paper 
15 
rates track very closely with changes in the Federal 
16 
Funds rate. 
17 
Q. 
Do changes in the Federal Funds rate also track with 
18 
changes in longer-term borrowing rates? 
19 
A. 
Yes, but another important factor impacting longer-term 
20 
borrowing rates is inflation.  While the Federal Reserve 
21 
continues to target an average inflation rate of 2%, it 
22 
has indicated a willingness to allow inflation to 
23 


--- Page 13 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 12 - 
moderately exceed 2% for some time. 
1 
Q. 
Has inflation exceeded the 2% target? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Inflation, as measured by changes in Personal 
3 
Consumption Expenditures (“PCE”), has been steadily 
4 
increasing for the past ten months.  For the period ended 
5 
September 2021, the PCE has had a 12-month average 
6 
increase of 2.8% and has exceeded 4.0% in each of the 
7 
last five months to reach a 30-year high of 4.4% in 
8 
September 2021. 
9 
Q. 
Is PCE the only measure of inflation? 
10 
A. 
No, there are other measures of inflation.  One of the 
11 
most common is the Consumer Price Index (“CPI”) which 
12 
measures the average monthly change in the price for 
13 
goods and services paid by urban consumers. 
14 
Q. 
Has the CPI also shown higher levels of inflation in 
15 
recent months? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  The CPI has risen from 0.2% in May 2021 to 6.8% in 
17 
November 2021, a level not seen since June 1982 (see 
18 
Exhibit__(YS-3)).     
19 
Q. 
What is the correlation between inflation and longer-term 
20 
borrowing rates? 
21 
A. 
A large component of long-term borrowing rates is 
22 
Treasury rates.  Treasury rates are sensitive to both 
23 


--- Page 14 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 13 - 
actual and forecasted inflation rates.  Higher inflation 
1 
rates drive higher Treasury rates and correspondingly 
2 
drive higher longer-term borrowing rates. 
3 
Q. 
Have you examined the historical relationship between the 
4 
rate of inflation and U.S. Treasury rates? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  In the scatter plot below, we charted the monthly, 
6 
year-over-year percentage change of the U.S. CPI (as the 
7 
independent variable) against the 10-year U.S. Treasury 
8 
yield (as the dependent variable) over the last 20 years.  
9 
This plot confirms that, over the past 20 years, there 
10 
has been a strong positive correlation between the rate 
11 
of inflation and U.S. Treasury yields as evidenced by the 
12 
coefficient of determination or R-squared of 0.76.  It is 
13 
reasonable to expect that as inflation increases, the 
14 
yield investors would demand on U.S. Treasury securities 
15 
will also increase. 
16

7

8

DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 15 - 
Q. 
What are potential reasons why 2021 has produced this 
1 
number of outliers in the data? 
2 
A. 
First, as discussed earlier, the Federal Reserve is 
3 
intervening in the fixed income market to keep interest 
4 
rates artificially low.  Second, the Federal Reserve’s 
5 
earlier expectations of inflation have turned out to be 
6 
incorrect.  At the April 2021 FOMC meeting, the Federal 
7 
Reserve’s press release stated: 
8 
 
 
9 
 
Inflation has risen, largely reflecting transitory 
10 
factors. 
11 
 
12 
 
Federal Reserve Chair Powell further added: 
13 
 
14 
 
An episode of one-time price increases as the 
15 
economy reopens is not likely to lead to persistent 
16 
year-over-year inflation into the future. 
17 
 
18 
 
But, over the past few month as inflation continues to 
19 
rise, the Federal Reserve has begun to acknowledge that 
20 
their initial expectations for inflation were wrong.  
21 
When Federal Reserve Chair Powell was asked, at a 
22 
congressional hearing in early December 2021, if he 
23 


--- Page 17 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 16 - 
thought that price increases were not particularly large 
1 
or persistent, he said: 
2 
 
3 
 
 
No, that is no longer my view. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
What implication could this change in the Federal 
6 
Reserve’s view on inflation have on interest rates? 
7 
A. 
The primary implication is that the Federal Reserve’s 
8 
error in initially viewing inflation as transitory has 
9 
caused it to delay reducing monetary stimulus and raising 
10 
interest rates to combat rising inflation.  If inflation 
11 
continues to remain high, the Federal Reserve may be 
12 
forced to act more forcefully.  In fact, in its December 
13 
FOMC meeting, the Federal Reserve announced that 
14 
beginning in January 2022 it would increase the pace of 
15 
tapering of its bond buying program by buying $60 billion 
16 
of bonds each month starting in January, half the level 
17 
prior to the November taper and $30 billion less than it 
18 
had been buying in December.  The Federal Reserve further 
19 
announced that “similar reductions in the pace of net 
20 
asset purchases will likely be appropriate each month”.  
21 
Additionally, a majority of the FOMC members indicated 
22 
that up to three 25 bps increases in the Federal Funds 
23 


--- Page 18 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 17 - 
rate may be required in 2022, with 12 of the 18 members 
1 
signaling that a target rate of at least 0.875% would be 
2 
appropriate. 
3 
Q. 
Is inflation expected to continue above the Federal 
4 
Reserve’s target of 2.0%? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  Multiple sources project the inflation rate to 
6 
remain elevated above the Federal Reserve’s target of 
7 
2.0% at least through the end of 2022 and into 2023.  The 
8 
November 2021 Blue Chip Financial Forecast has both the 
9 
CPI and PCE indices decreasing from their current highs, 
10 
but remaining above 2.0% through the forecast period 
11 
ended Q1 2023 (see Exhibit__(YS-4)).  
12 
Q. 
Could inflation continue at a rate higher than 2.0% after 
13 
Q1 2023? 
14 
A. 
It is highly possible.  Two factors currently impacting 
15 
inflation are historically high government spending and 
16 
historically low interest rates and while the Fed has 
17 
recently started to talk about the potential need to 
18 
raise rates in 2022, the high level of government 
19 
spending shows no indication of ending in the near 
20 
future.  Since May 2020 the Federal government has passed 
21 
COVID-19 relief stimulus packages totaling $5.8 trillion.  
22 
Additionally, in November 2021, the $1.2 trillion 
23 


--- Page 19 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 18 - 
Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act was signed into 
1 
law and Congress is considering whether to enact 
2 
additional spending legislation, such as the Build Back 
3 
Better Act.   
4 
Q. 
Do the Federal Reserve Board members expect inflation to 
5 
remain above 2.0% over the next several years? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  In their December 2021 economic projections, the 
7 
median of the Federal Reserve Board members’ expectation 
8 
of PCE inflation was 2.6% in 2022, 2.3% in 2023 and 2.1% 
9 
in 2024. 
10 
Q. 
Has the Company proposed a method to mitigate risks of 
11 
inflation on interest rates? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Accounting Panel testimony describes a proposal 
13 
to reconcile the effects of inflation to the extent that 
14 
actual inflation exceeds 5.0% in any of the rate years 
15 
and the Company’s electric or gas earnings are less than 
16 
the authorized ROE applicable to that rate year. 
17 
Q. 
Is inflation the only factor in today’s financial markets 
18 
that could significantly impact the Company’s cost of 
19 
capital? 
20 
A. 
No, volatility in the financial markets has been and will 
21 
continue to be one of the Company’s most significant 
22 
challenges as the Company continually needs to access the 
23 


--- Page 20 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 19 - 
capital markets.  Global events like the COVID-19 
1 
pandemic have the potential to further increase 
2 
volatility in the capital markets.  Other impactful 
3 
geopolitical events (e.g., trade tensions between the 
4 
U.S. and China, unrest surrounding the U.S. political 
5 
environment, and the ongoing threat of a shutdown of the 
6 
Federal government) can also produce shocks that could 
7 
affect the Company’s ability to access capital markets 
8 
efficiently. 
9 
Q. 
How will the possibility of rising interest rates in a 
10 
potentially volatile market impact the Company?  
11 
A. 
Taking the aforementioned factors into account, one of 
12 
the main challenges faced by the Company is its ability 
13 
to earn a fair rate of return.  A confluence of factors, 
14 
including the regulatory approach in New York to setting 
15 
cost rates for debt and equity and a rising interest rate 
16 
environment, expose the Company to the risk that it will 
17 
not be able to earn its cost of capital. This will make 
18 
the Company less attractive to both equity and debt 
19 
investors which, in turn, could increase the costs of 
20 
financing. 
21 
Q. 
Is there evidence that these factors have already 
22 
impacted the Company’s attractiveness to investors? 
23 


--- Page 21 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 20 - 
A. 
Yes. Equity investors have historically given the Company 
1 
a lower valuation than its peer groups.  When compared to 
2 
other companies in the S&P 500, Con Edison, Inc.’s 
3 
(“CEI”) five-year average price to book ratio ranks in 
4 
the bottom quartile.  Also, CEI’s level of positive 
5 
equity analyst recommendations ranked 503 out of the 505 
6 
S&P 500 members that have rankings available. 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the shortcomings of New York’s approach 
8 
to setting cost rates for debt in the current financial 
9 
market environment? 
10 
A. 
The Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) sets 
11 
cost rates for fixed debt based only on current interest 
12 
rates, which ignores the risk of rising rates as the 
13 
Federal Reserve continues to aim for higher sustained 
14 
inflation targets.  In addition, rates have exhibited 
15 
volatility in relation to recent economic uncertainty.  
16 
Since the beginning of 2020, there have been close to 140 
17 
basis point swings between the high and low yields for 
18 
both 10-year and 30-year Treasury bonds.  A rise in 
19 
volatility would likely lead investors to require a 
20 
higher rate of return to compensate them for the 
21 
additional risks that they will have to bear given this 
22 
increased volatility.   
23 


--- Page 22 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 21 - 
Q. 
Has the Company proposed a method to mitigate risks of 
1 
interest rate volatility? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  As described in more detail in Section II of this 
3 
testimony, the primary method to mitigate the risks of 
4 
future interest rate volatility is to use a forward 
5 
looking method to forecast interest rates rather than 
6 
using only current rates as a proxy.  However, since 
7 
Staff has historically not accepted this forecast 
8 
methodology, the Accounting Panel testimony describes a 
9 
proposal, similar to the existing true-up of the weighted 
10 
average cost of variable debt, to true up the weighted 
11 
average cost of long term debt to the fixed debt cost 
12 
rates reflected in Exhibit AP-5  
13 
Q. 
Please describe the shortcomings with Staff’s approach to 
14 
setting cost rates for equity in the current financial 
15 
market environment. 
16 
A. 
The current low interest rate environment has pushed 
17 
utility equity market valuations above historical levels.  
18 
These conditions are exacerbating the flaws of Staff’s 
19 
reliance on a formulaic approach to determining a fair 
20 
return on equity (“ROE”).  Staff’s discounted cash flow 
21 
(“DCF”) model, in particular, is producing results that 
22 
are well below historical levels. 
23

1 
II. 
CAPITALIZATION AND COST OF CAPITAL 
2 
Q.  What capital structure should be used in the context of 
3 
these rate case proceedings? 
4 
A. 
A capital structure with a 50.00% equity ratio, 0.89% 
5 
customer deposits ratio and a 49.11% debt ratio should be 
6 
used.  
7 
Q. 
Please describe why this proposed capital structure is 
8 
appropriate. 
9 
A. 
The proposed capital structure with a 50.00% equity ratio 
10 
(as compared with the 48.00% equity ratio in the 
11 
Company’s current electric and gas rate plans) is 
12 
appropriate and necessary to address the Company’s weaker 
13 
cash flow profile.  The Company’s weaker cash flow 
14 
profile is a direct result of the continued low ROE and 
15 
equity ratios in its recent rate plans.  
16 
Q. 
Have there been other factors that have contributed to 
17 
the Company’s weaker cash flows? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  The passage of the Tax Cut and Jobs Act of 2017 
19 
(“TCJA”), the COVID-19 pandemic, and the impact from 
20 
Tropical Storm Isaias, have all recently contributed to 
21 
the Company’s weaker cash flows. 
22 


--- Page 24 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 23 - 
Q. 
What impact did the TCJA have on the Company’s cash 
1 
flows? 
2 
A. 
The passage of the TCJA in 2017 exacerbated the Company’s 
3 
already weak cash flows. The reduction of the maximum 
4 
corporate tax rate from 35% to 21% and the curtailment of 
5 
bonus depreciation required the Company to return a total 
6 
of $2,823 million to electric customers ($377 million 
7 
over 3 years, $784 million over 5 years and $1,663 
8 
million over the remaining lives of the related assets) 
9 
and $895 million to gas customers from 2020 onward ($63 
10 
million over 2 years, $107 million over 5 years and $725 
11 
million over the remaining lives of the related assets), 
12 
negatively affecting its cash flow.  
13 
Q. 
What impact has the COVID-19 pandemic had on the 
14 
Company’s cash flows?  
15 
A. 
In March 2020, the Company began suspending service 
16 
disconnections, certain collection notices, final bill 
17 
collection agency activity, new late payment charges and 
18 
certain other fees for all customers. The Company also 
19 
began providing payment extensions for all customers that 
20 
were scheduled to be disconnected prior to the start of 
21 
the COVID-19 pandemic. In June 2020, the State of New 
22 
York enacted a law prohibiting New York utilities, 
23 


--- Page 25 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 24 - 
including Con Edison, from disconnecting residential 
1 
customers during the first COVID-19 pandemic state of 
2 
emergency. Subsequent legislation extended protections to 
3 
certain small commercial customers during the first state 
4 
of emergency.   The Company had foregone an estimated 
5 
$124 million of revenue in late payment charges and other 
6 
fees and increased its uncollectible account reserve by 
7 
$198 million due to these actions.  Although recovery has 
8 
recently begun for the unbilled 2020 late payment fees, 
9 
the delay in recovery put cash flow pressure on the 
10 
Company. 
11 
Q. 
What impact did Tropical Storm Isaias have on the 
12 
Company’s cash flows?  
13 
A. 
Con Edison incurred costs for Tropical Storm Isaias of 
14 
$153 million, including $77 million of incremental 
15 
operation and maintenance costs.  Delay in recovery of 
16 
expenses incurred under severe storms like Tropical Storm 
17 
Isaias puts the Company at a cash flow disadvantage.  As 
18 
storm events continue to increase in both frequency and 
19 
intensity, they will continue to erode the cash position 
20 
of the Company.  
21

22 
expenses?

8

10

11

12 
ending December 31, 2023, respectively.  These schedules 
13

14

15

16

17

18

19 
average cost of long-term debt.

Q. 
What method have you used to develop the interest rate 
1 
forecasts?  
2 
A. 
The Company has used forecasts of Treasury bond rates 
3 
from the publication Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, plus 
4 
a spread to Treasury bond rates based on indicative 
5 
quotes from financial institutions.  The Blue Chip 
6 
Financial Forecasts consist of the consensus forecast of 
7 
approximately 45 economists.  This approach provides more 
8 
reasonable forecast results than simply using the most 
9 
current Treasury bond rates.  At the update stage of this 
10 
proceeding, the Company will revise Exhibit AP-5, 
11 
Schedule 6, to reflect the most recent data available, as 
12 
well as any new or refinanced debt that the Company may 
13 
have issued by that time. 
14 
Q.

15

16 
A.

17

18 
estimate of future long-term interest rates relies on 
19 
academic papers that the Company believes are not 
20 
relevant.   
21

22 
A. 
Yes. In the direct testimony of the Staff Finance Panel 
23 


--- Page 47 ---

(p. 49) submitted in the most recent Orange and Rockland 
1

2

3 
 
4 
…relatively short-term movements in long-term 
5 
interest rates are difficult to forecast. Such 
6

7

8

9

10 
of future long-term interest rates is no-change; in 
11 
other words, the current rates of these debt 
12

19

“Professional Forecasts of Interest Rates: Evidence from

the Wall Street Journal’s Panel of Economists” by Karlyn

The Company believes that these papers are not relevant

15 
An article called, “Interest Rate Forecasts in 
16 
Conventional and Unconventional Monetary Policy Periods” 
17 
by Nelson Oliver and Mehmet Pasaogullari published by the 
18 
Federal Reserve Bank of Cleveland in May, 2015 
19 
(Exhibit__(YS-14)) states:  
20 
  
21 
 
Monetary policy has been conducted with a different 
22 
set of tools since the financial crisis, and we 
23 


--- Page 49 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 48 - 
investigate whether the change has affected the 
1 
accuracy of professionals’ interest-rate forecasts. 
2 
We analyze the accuracy of federal funds rate and 
3 
nominal Treasury yield forecasts in the periods 
4 
before and after the introduction of new policy 
5 
tools and find that, in general, forecast accuracy 
6 
improved in the latter policy period. 
7 
 
8 
The source of forecasts for the analysis used in this 
9

10 
that is used by the Company. 
 
11 
Q. 
What is a better method than using current rates to 
12 
forecast rates? 
13 
A. 
A forward looking measure of rates is a better 
14 
forecasting method.  Examples of forward looking measures 
15 
are the forward rate curve or a consensus of economists’ 
16 
estimates contained in the Blue Chip Financial Forecasts. 
17 
The forward rate is the rate you can lock in today to 
18 
borrow in the future and can be interpreted as the 
19 
market’s consensus forecast of interest rates.  A 
20 
consensus forecast of Treasury rates, such as that 
21 
produced by Blue Chip Financial, provides a more 
22 
reasonable estimate rather than simply relying on current 
23 


--- Page 50 ---

rates.

15 
III. 
CAPITAL NEEDS AND INVESTOR CONCERNS 
16 
Q. 
Please describe the financial challenges facing the 
17 
Company during the Rate Year and beyond. 
18 
A.  The Company faces the following interrelated financial 
19 
challenges: (A) the capital intensive nature of its 
20 
business; (B) the costs of implementing clean energy 
21 
projects pursuant to government and regulatory authority 
22 
policies to fight climate change and improve resilience 
23 


--- Page 54 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 53 - 
to extreme weather; (C) flat demand growth for 
1 
electricity; (D) its unusually weak cash flows; (E) the 
2 
restrictions that regulation places on its ability to 
3 
respond to unfavorable developments in its environment, 
4 
and (F) its dependence on the market to fund its capital 
5 
needs.  Each of these challenges is discussed further 
6 
below. 
7 
Q. 
Please discuss the capital intensive nature of the 
8 
Company’s business. 
9 
A. 
The Company’s business requires significant capital 
10 
investment every year, its assets are long-lived and the 
11 
underlying technology, facilities and customer base are 
12 
mature. 
Capital intensity is high for utilities.  
13 
According to a June 2, 2011, IHS Cambridge Energy 
14 
Research Associates presentation titled "Post Fukushima: 
15 
If not nuclear, what energy mix?, the electric utility 
16 
industry is the most capital intensive industry as 
17 
measured by the ratio of total assets to total revenues.  
18 
As shown on Exhibit___(YS-15), which was prepared under 
19 
my supervision and direction, the Company’s capital 
20 
intensity can be demonstrated by the fact that its ratio 
21 
of net fixed assets per dollar of revenues is 3.7, as 
22 
compared with 2.9 for the average S&P 500 company and 0.2 
23 


--- Page 55 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 54 - 
for the median company.  Capital intensity amplifies risk 
1 
for investors because capital intensive businesses have 
2 
to recover much larger fixed costs (interest and 
3 
depreciation) before achieving a return on their 
4 
investment.  Even factoring in the Climate Leadership and 
5 
Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”), the Company’s assets 
6 
also have extraordinarily long lives.  Long-lived assets, 
7 
in the context of rate regulation, present two financial 
8 
challenges for the Company that are also risks for 
9 
potential investors in the Company’s debt issuances and 
10 
equity shares.  First, their investment horizons for 
11 
capital recovery must be much longer.  For debt 
12 
investors, utility debt has much longer average 
13 
maturities than other companies.  Equity investors must 
14 
also wait longer for repayment on their investment.  
15 
Second, there is a regulatory risk in long-lived assets 
16 
because United States rate regulation limits returns to a 
17 
fraction of historic tangible book value rather than 
18 
replacement or current market value.  The Company’s 
19

20 
net book values, are small relative to its current 
21 
capital costs, returning only 47% of its capital 
22 
expenditures in the form of depreciation for the twelve 
23

months ended December 31, 2020. 
1

2

3

4 
supervision and direction, by way of comparison, the 
5

6

7

12 
which was prepared under my supervision and direction. 
13 
The average recovery rate for the utility companies in 
14 
the S&P 500 was 62%. 
15 
 
The Company’s large installed base of mature equipment 
16 
requires a continuous investment in replacement assets.  
17 
In other industries, a much larger portion of investment 
18 
can be dedicated to new business (generating offsetting 
19 
revenues) or new technology (lowering costs). 
20 
 
Mature assets raise operating costs and increase 
21 
operating risks, particularly in an environment that 
22 
requires high levels of reliability and imposes virtually 
23 


--- Page 57 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 56 - 
automatic regulatory penalties for failing to achieve it.  
1 
The technology of the business is also mature, affording 
2 
little opportunity to significantly reduce invested 
3 
capital in the business through technological innovation.  
4 
The need for continuous investment to maintain and 
5 
improve the system with slight opportunities for demand 
6 
growth and limited depreciation cash flow means that the 
7 
Company must seek rate increases and raise new capital 
8 
frequently to maintain its financial stability.  
9 
Replacement capital needs alone substantially exceed the 
10 
cash generated through depreciation recoveries for the 
11 
Company.

Q.

4

17

19

21

22 
Commission routinely requires earnings sharing

23

Q.

8

9 
A. 
No.  The priority of bondholders’ claim on the Company 
10

11 
risk.  Shareholders, unlike bondholders, only have a 
12 
residual claim to the resources and income of the 
13 
Company, and thus face risks even in well-rated 
14 
companies.  If returns are inadequate, the bondholder may 
15 
suffer a loss on paper from a credit downgrade.  The 
16 
stockholder will suffer the loss directly through a drop 
17 
in the share price and/or through a lower dividend. 
18 
Q. 
Why do companies such as Con Edison need to maintain a 
19 
particularly strong financial condition? 
20 
A. 
Capital intensive companies with a statutory obligation 
21 
to serve have to borrow despite the state of the market 
22 
because they need continuous access to capital. In 
23 


--- Page 64 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
DIRECT TESTIMONY - YUKARI SAEGUSA 
 
- 63 - 
addition, utilities may have to access the capital market 
1 
in response to a natural catastrophe e.g., Superstorm 
2 
Sandy.  When utilities are forced to pay high rates, 
3 
these rates will remain with the companies and their 
4

5

6

7

8

9 
in all markets. Such access has become more tenuous for 
10 
Con Edison due to its current A-2/P-2 (S&P’s/ Moody’s) 
11 
rating for commercial paper.  At the height of the 
12 
financial crisis of 2008-2009, non-A-1/P-1 borrowers, if 
13 
they had access to commercial paper market, paid 
14 
significantly higher rates. The same was true in the 
15 
early days of the COVID-19 pandemic where A2/P2 
16 
commercial paper peaked at 3.61% on March 26, 2020 versus 
17 
an average of 1.76% for the pre-pandemic months of 
18 
January and February 2020. 
19 
 
During both times, the seizing up of the commercial paper

market was relieved only by the Federal government’s 
21

highest-rated (A-1/P-1) commercial paper issuers, a 
23 


--- Page 65 ---

2

3

4

Q. 
How do equity investors in regulated utilities set their 
1 
expectations for compensation? 
2 
A. 
The return expectations of equity investors in rate-
3 
regulated energy utilities are grounded in “the 
4 
regulatory compact.”  The regulatory compact’s essence is 
5 
that equity investors forgo the monopoly earnings they 
6 
would otherwise enjoy in return for the 
7 
institutionalization of their monopoly in a defined 
8 
geographic area and a fair and equitable return on the 
9 
capital they have invested. 
10 
Q. 
What standards exist to help equity investors and 
11 
regulators determine whether a rate-regulated utility 
12 
offers a fair and equitable return?  
13 
A. 
The general standards for a fair and equitable 
14 
return for investors in utility shares are well-
15 
established in the U.S.  The underlying requirement 
16 
for fair treatment for equity investors has been 
17 
recognized for years.  As discussed in the direct 
18 
testimony of Company witness Villadsen, it dates 
19 
back to the Hope and Bluefield cases.  The U.S. 
20 
Supreme Court in those cases established that in 
21 
determining the fairness or reasonableness of a 
22 
utility’s allowed ROE, one needed to look at the 
23 


--- Page 69 ---

1

2 
having similar or comparable risks.

IV. 
CONCLUSION 
1 
Q. 
Please summarize your testimony regarding the 
2 
financial challenges facing the Company. 
3 
A. 
My testimony concerns the financial challenges and the 
4 
need to maintain access to financial markets at 
5 
reasonable cost.  Both equity and debt investors 
6 
perceive that the New York regulatory environment is a 
7 
difficult one in which to operate.  Such a perception, 
8 
if it continues, will make the financing of needed 
9 
expenditures more expensive in normal times and less 
10 
certain in times of financial crises. 
11 
 
To avoid such an outcome, and to re-establish debt and 
12 
equity investors’ trust in the fairness of New York 
13 
regulation, a fair and equitable rate of return, 
14 
competitive with those available elsewhere in the 
15 
market, and a reasonable chance to actually earn that 
16 
return, are needed.  And to achieve such, the 
17 
Commission should grant the rate of return and capital 
18 
structure requested by the Company. 
19 
Q. 
Does that conclude your direct testimony? 
20 
A. 
Yes, it does.

7
Will the Fed raise the RRP and IOER rates?
• Despite the low level of the funds rate within its target bands, the Fed did not raise
either the RRP or IOER rates in April
• The April FOMC minutes suggest what the Fed would need to see before raising
these rates and attempting to guide the funds rate away from the range floor
• There would need to be dislocations in short-term rate markets
• And trading volumes in the funds market would have to decline
• Although these conditions were not in place last month, they may crop up later this
year as rising excess reserves push the funds rate closer to zero

FHLBs now account for most of the lending in fed funds…

lending in the fed funds market
• Although they don’t earn IOER, they can

unremunerated account
• Or in the RRP program also at 0%
• As the funds rate approaches zero, they
may simply decide to leave their cash at
the Fed
• And stop selling cash into the funds
market causing volumes to plunge

16
Will the Fed raise the OMO rate?
• In April, the FOMC discussed increasing the rate on the OMOs “relative to IOER”
• The intention is to make the program more of a backstop for the repo market
• But does this mean the Fed will take the OMO rate above IOER?
• Our sense is that the Fed will leave the rate at 10bp for now
• It is not clear what the Fed would accomplish by pushing the rate above IOER
• The Fed has already reduced the frequency of its term operations
• And balances are falling as market rates are cheaper than the 10bp offered
in the daily OMOs

18
Mid-June spike?
• The 3m central bank dollar swaps start to roll off in mid-June
• Roughly $225bn – or nearly half – of the outstanding balances will mature
between June 11 and 18
• But does this mean cross-currency spreads will widen?1/
• Our sense is that spreads will not widen
• The availability of bank liquidity has improved significantly since mid-March as
bank reserves have increased
• Counterparty risk fears have abated
• And unsecured dollar funding markets (such as CP and wholesale CDs) have
improved
1/ A recent Federal Reserve post concludes that the swap lines “were not associated with significant improvements in market functioning as initially term 
liquidity obtained by banks was only partially channeled beyond the banking system”. See “Have the Fed Swap Lines Reduced Dollar Funding Strains during 
the COVID-19 Outbreak?”, N. Cetorelli, L. Goldberg, and F. Ravazzolo, Federal Reserve Bank of New York, May 22, 2020.

How big will the Fed’s balance sheet get?

Negative rates
Exhibit__(YS-2)

--- Page 108 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
31
Fed policy and negative rates1/
• Fed Chair Powell noted recently that negative rate policy is “not an attractive
monetary policy tool”.
• And, that “for now it’s [negative rates] not something we are considering”
• This is consistent with Fed policy discussions across three different Fed chairs over
the last 10y
• While the Fed would never completely rule out using a policy instrument, our
sense is that pushing the target rate below zero is a very low probability
• But markets have started to price in some small chance of negative policy rates
• That reflect a distribution of economic recovery paths including those in which
the Fed’s current mix of tools are inadequate
1/ See Less than zero, May 21, 2020.
2/ See Fed Chair Powell: Significant downside risk to the outlook, but negative rates are not the answer, May 13, 2020. 
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 31 OF 69


--- Page 109 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
32
Can rates be taking below zero?
• As recently as 2016, there was some question about whether the Fed had the ability to
lower IOER into negative territory
• But in an economy with a super-abundance of bank reserves, the RRP rate – or the
effective interest rate floor – is more significant than IOER
• And repo rates frequently trade below zero1/
• So we see no legal reason why the Fed couldn’t take the fed funds rate below zero
1/ To be sure, repo rates for specific securities can trade below zero. General collateral rates – at least in the US – have traded above 0%. 
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 32 OF 69


--- Page 110 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
33
Mechanics of negative rates
• There are three things the Fed would need to do in order to push the fed funds rate
below zero:
1. Lower the RRP rate into negative territory
2. Reduce the remuneration rate on the GSE and foreign official institution balances
held at the Fed
•
This is set at 0% – if this rate is not lowered, these institutions would leave
their cash at the Fed
•
And trading in the fed funds market would dry up
3. Implement some form of reserve tiering in which a portion of bank reserves is
exempt from the negative IOER rate
• This would reduce the strain on banks from the large reserves balances they
hold as a result of the Fed’s balance sheet expansion
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 33 OF 69


--- Page 111 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
34
Implications of negative rates for money markets
• Negative interest rates would have significant consequences for market activity and
bank intermediation:
• Money market instruments are generally issued and sold at a discount and mature to
par
• Negative rates would turn this upside down – instruments would “de-accrete” to
par
• This would create significant problems for money funds
• As money funds use the “accretion to par” and historical cost accounting to
maintain stable NAVs
• How much money would leave money funds and where would it all go?
• Can banks pass along negative deposit rates?

Money funds have two options…
Gov-only fund balances ($bn)
Source: Crane’s Data, Barclays Research
• Stable NAV funds (gov-only) have two
ways to adapt to negative interest rates
• Float their NAVs
• Or cancel shares while maintaining
the stable NAV
• Some money fund investors are legally
required to keep their cash in stable NAV
funds
• Regardless, retail and institutional
investors both dislike either approach
and balances would flow out of money
funds

37
Reversal rate
• If banks are unable or unwilling to pass along negative interest rates to all their
depositors and deposit inflows accelerate as money flows out of money market funds,
what happens to their lending and asset growth?
• Reversal rate
• It is possible for interest rates to be so low that banks are unable to operate
normally and they pull back from lending
• Policy becomes unintentionally tighter as a result
• The ECB adopted reserve tiering last October in order to minimize the drag on banks
caused by negative interest rates

Bank reserves ($bn)

• Bank reserves are concentrated at the
largest US banks
• The Fed eliminated required reserves in
March1/
• The ECB determines the exclusion
base as a multiple of the bank’s
reserves
• The Fed would need to consider
an alternative exclusion amount
• Tiering could create other market
distortions

52
Libor transition survey1/
• Barclays Research conducted its second survey of the Libor transition in the first two
weeks of May, with a focus on the effect of COVID-19 on the transition
• Only a small portion of respondents felt that COVID-19 would significantly affect
the Libor transition timeline
• Recent volatility in SOFR has renewed interest in a credit-sensitive spread
adjustment for the risk-free benchmark rate
• A plurality of respondents view the creation of the Credit Sensitivity Group (CSG)
by the Fed positively
• And felt the transition timeline should be delayed until the CSG releases
its findings
1/ See Libor transition survey: COVID-19 is having an effect, May 20, 2020

56
Credit Sensitivity Group
• Several months ago, the Fed and other bank regulators agreed to create a Credit
Sensitivity Group (CSG).
• CSG will examine the needs of lenders and borrowers who may find that a risk-
free reference rate such as SOFR is not sufficient for their needs.
• And while the CSG is new, the issue of incorporating credit risk into the
benchmark replacement for Libor has been circulating for several years1/
1/ See “Reforming Libor and Other Financial Benchmarks”, D. Duffie and J. Stein, Stanford and Harvard University working paper, September 19, 2014

May 26, 2020
58
Bank asset and liability mismatch concerns
• The recent widening in the spread between Libor and SOFR has renewed concerns
about a potential mismatch between bank assets and liabilities that could emerge
during a financial shock
• In a Libor world, the reference rate underlying bank assets and liabilities is the
same
• A financial market shock would push both sides of the balance sheet in the
same direction
• Bank funding costs rise but so to do bank revenues
• But in a financial crisis SOFR may rally, creating a mismatch between the bank’s
funding costs and its revenues
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 58 OF 69


--- Page 136 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
59
COVID-19 reference rate mismatch
Libor less term SOFR (bp)
Source: Bloomberg, Federal Reserve, Barclays Research
• In the early stages of the pandemic, Libor
and bank unsecured funding costs
surged
• While term SOFR rallied
• This caused the 3m spread between the
reference rates to widen to 140bp
• Survey respondents indicated that their
number one concern about the Libor
transition was the need for a credit
sensitive benchmark1/

--- Page 137 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
60
Adding a credit spread: Bank Yield Index
• One approach to address the asset-liability mismatch is to add a dynamic credit spread
to term SOFR
• The credit spread adjustment would widen in a financial crisis – just like Libor
• And the rates underlying bank assets and liabilities would each move in the
same direction
• Bank Yield Index (BYI)1/
• BYI adds a credit spread to term SOFR that is based on primary and secondary
market corporate (bank) bond transactions
• As this data is thin, the BYI sums activity over the prior 5d
• Subject to minimum size and transactions volume thresholds
• These transactions are put into a regression to fit a curve that
calculates 1m, 3m, and 6m spreads
• These are then added to the corresponding term SOFR rate
1/ See ICE Benchmark Administration Publishes Fourth Update Regarding the U.S. Dollar ICE Bank Yield Index, May 6, 2020
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 60 OF 69


--- Page 138 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
61
BYI looks like Libor
• Over the 3y sample period (2017-2020) the BYI is very similar to Libor
• The median daily spread between 3m Libor and 3m BYI is 2bp
• And the median absolutes spread (without regard to sign) is 2.7bp
• But even with the 5d observation window, volume is still thin (between $20-30bn)
• And it introduces a non-current credit spread to a term SOFR rate that is adjusting
much faster
• Most of the underlying data come from the market for unsecured deposits
• This market is not very transparent nor is transaction data published with the same
level of detail as SOFR
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 61 OF 69


--- Page 139 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
62
BYI questions
• BYI uses issuance data from 14 of the 16 Libor panel member banks for the testing
phase
• But will banks agree to supply their issuance data to construct BYI after Libor
publication ceases?
• How will BYI be calculated if, term SOFR is not robust or IOSCO compliant by the
December 2021 deadline?
• The credit spread is forward looking but markets may be using compounded SOFR
in arrears
Exhibit__(YS-2)
PAGE 62 OF 69


--- Page 140 ---
Restricted - External
May 26, 2020
63
Ameribor
• Instead of adding a credit spread adjustment to term SOFR, Ameribor uses futures
contracts to construct a wholly transactions based measure of unsecured bank funding
costs
• Futures are based on bank loans cleared through an inter-bank platform
maintained by the American Financial Exchange1/,2/
• The Ameribor rate is a weighted average of transactions on this platform. Daily
activity across the roughly 180 participants is around $2bn/day
• Bank loans are for overnight and 30d tenors
1/ See Ameribor Methodology, AFX
2/ The futures contracts are for 7d or 3m. They use the compounded in arrears daily Ameribor rate
Exhibit__(YS-2)

May 26, 2020
65
Multiple benchmarks
• The Alternative Reference Rate Committee notes that investors are free to use any
benchmark
• And that they are not obligated to use SOFR
• Indeed, historically, there have been multiple benchmarks for different types of activity
• Including:
• Constant maturity Treasury yields
• And a bank cost of funding index
• Even though Libor was the dominant benchmark
• We expect multiple benchmarks to emerge post-Libor
• Even though SOFR is likely to become the dominant reference rate
• And the dominant instrument used for hedging risk

. 
Fed Expected to Taper Asset Purchases; Inflation Expected to Moderate 
The Federal Open Market Committee will meet Tuesday and 
Wednesday this week, November 2 and 3.  As is typical pres-
ently, the Committee is not expected to alter the federal funds 
rate – indeed, the Blue Chip Financial Forecasts panel doesn’t 
see that move until late in 2022, according to this month’s 
Special Questions on page 14 and the forecasts on pages 2, 8 
and 9.  But at this meeting, the FOMC is expected to announce 
the beginning of a tapering of its asset purchase program.  The 
panel believes that process will take about eight months, ac-
cording to another Special Question, so it would conclude be-
fore any interest rate action might be taken. 
Supply-Side Forces Dominate. The U.S. economy is current-
ly plagued by a mismatch of supply and demand forces that 
can’t easily be mitigated by the application of conventional 
policy tools.  Attempting to pare back the recent climb in infla-
tion via tighter monetary policy may not be that effective as 
those price pressures have been partly driven by supply-side 
forces and not necessarily by loose monetary policy.  Evidence 
of overheating, for instance, is not that acute.  Indeed, industri-
al capacity utilization was 75.2% in September and historically 
it has often been more than 80% before corrective policies 
were deemed appropriate.  The Labor Department reports that 
there were 10.4 million job openings in August, up from 6.8 
million at the end of last year, in the face of a labor force par-
ticipation rate of just 61.6%, down from 63.4% in early 2020, 
just before the pandemic struck, and more, roundly 67% just 
before the turn of the 21st century.  It may not be prudent to 
discourage job seekers and job providers at such a time as 
now. 
Broader growth and inflation forces are prevalent in other 
countries as well, as discussed on page 3 of this report. 
COVID Still a Key Factor in Policy and Financial Mar-
kets. Part of the disconcerting aspects of recent economic per-
formance comes from the persistence of the COVID pandemic. 
Cases per million people in the U.S. have decreased markedly, 
but at 217 per million people as of October 27, they remain 
well above the 40 per million that prevailed back in June and 
July.  This too seems to present good reason for not exerting 
more pressure through monetary policy. 
At the same time, the Blue Chip panelists seem to believe that 
growth will be orderly in coming quarters and that inflation 
will moderate.  GDP growth is forecast to be 4.6% in Q4 2021 
and ease moderately toward 2.6% by late 2022.  Inflation, per-
haps more importantly, is expected to be 3.4% in Q4, meas-
ured by the personal consumption expenditure price index 
targeted by the Federal Reserve in its policy-making.  Inflation 
is then forecast to slow to 2.2% by late next year.  As ex-
plained above, these growth and inflation outcomes are ex-
pected in the absence of any aggressive monetary tightening.  
Could Inflation Linger?  Still, there is some skepticism from 
the Blue Chip panel.  According to associated Special Ques-
tions, panelists believe that current inflationary conditions are 
“likely to linger” rather than being “temporary.”  The situation  
this time is marked by supply-chain issues, which are seen by 
the largest share of the panel as generating the current “upside 
inflation risk.”  The panel is almost evenly split about whether 
financial markets are too complacent about the inflation risks; 
53% say markets are too complacent while 47% don’t believe 
that.  Among central banks, the panel worries that the Federal 
Reserve might be too complacent, while other central banks, 
especially the Bank of Japan, the Bank of England and the 
Bank of Canada, are more concerned and likely to act.  But the 
biggest threat to global economic stability is still seen as 
COVID and an uneven vaccine rollout, with supply chain con-
cerns next in order of destabilizing the global economy. 
Budget Deficit Still Huge, but Down from FY2020.  Fiscal 
policy, prior to the enactment of current legislation in process 
in the Congress, is summarized by the Blue Chip panel’s re-
sponse to another Special Question, in generating a budget 
deficit of $1.5 trillion in fiscal year 2022, which runs to Sep-
tember 2022.  The deficit was $2.772 billion in fiscal year 
2021, which ended on September 30 and equal to 12.4% of 
GDP.  This was down from $3.129 billion in fiscal 2020 or 
14.9% of GDP.  The fiscal 2022 deficit would be about 6% of 
nominal GDP, according to the panel’s current forecast. 
While obviously much smaller than in FY20 and FY21, that 
6% amount would still be the largest since FY2012.  The panel 
believes the FY2022 deficit would support growth, but not put 
undue upward pressure on interest rates or inflation. 
+ + + + +
SOFR Forecast Preview 
As we have explained in the last two months, LIBOR interest 
rates will be discontinued at the end of this year.  The new 
benchmark rate for short-term private sector borrowing activi-
ty will be the “secured overnight financing rate” or “SOFR.” 
Here is a summary of this month’s SOFR forecasts by panel-
ists.  As people become accustomed to working with this rate, 
more Blue Chip participants are submitting these forecasts, so 
from 19 panelists last month, this month there are 23.

Forecasts of panel members are on pages 10 and 11. Definitions of vari-
ables are as follows:  1Monetary policy rates. 2Government bonds are 
yields to maturity. 3Foreign exchange rate forecasts for U.K., Australia 
and the Euro are U.S. dollars per currency unit. For the U.S dollar, 
forecasts are of the U.S. Federal Reserve Board’s AFE Dollar Index. 
International. Notwithstanding some slowing in new Delta-variant 
cases, the outlook for global economic activity continues to soften as 
inflation continues to pick up. The combination of slowing growth and 
rising inflation has raised the specter of stagflation in the minds of 
some market participants. In the 1970s, a number of economies expe-
rienced elevated and rising unemployment along with elevated and 
rising inflation. Some might wonder if this could be occurring again. 
To be sure, the pace of economic growth is slowing, but from unsus-
tainably high rates that reflected the bounce back from pandemic-
related lockdowns and the ongoing reopening of economies. Even 
with this slowdown, rates of current and expected economic growth 
remain well above economies’ potential rates and importantly, unem-
ployment is continuing to decline. It is hard to call the current eco-
nomic outlook one of stagnation, but rather one of growth slowing 
from an unsustainable pace. 
As for inflation, much of the recent acceleration reflects relative price 
changes, particularly an increase in goods prices relative to services 
prices as demand during the pandemic rotated away from services 
toward goods. The goods-producing sectors were caught off guard as 
production processes were slow to respond to a much larger- and 
quicker-than-expected rebound in demand. The inability to supply the 
sharp rise in demand has also been exacerbated by supply-chain bot-
tlenecks and just-in-time inventory strategies. The end result has been 
a significant rise in goods prices which has also pushed up the overall 
rate of inflation. However, goods supply will eventually catch up with 
goods demand and the rotation from services to goods will run its 
course. So, it is unlikely that these conditions will generate a sustained 
acceleration in overall inflation. 
Indeed, financial markets are presently expecting the acceleration in 
inflation to be temporary. The modest increase in market-based infla-
tion expectations recently makes it difficult to conclude that expecta-
tions have become unanchored. Analysts’ and central banks’ forecasts 
generally see inflation receding by the end of this year and throughout 
2022. Longer-term market interest rates (that are very sensitive to 
changes in inflation and inflation expectations) have risen some over 
2021. Since the beginning of this year, the largest increase in longer-
term yields in the countries in our forecast sample has been a 90-basis-
point gain in the yield on the 10-year UK government note. For all ten 
countries in our forecast sample, the average increase has been 51 
basis points since the beginning of 2021. By comparison, the annual 
rate of consumer price inflation has increased 2-4 percentage points 
over this period. Looking ahead, our forecast panel consensus expects 
only a gradual additional increase in longer-term yields over the com-
ing year, averaging up 31 basis points from current levels for the ten 
countries, hardly an indication of expected raging inflation. 
While the response of developed market (DM) central banks to the 
surge in inflation so far has mostly been verbal, central banks of 
emerging market (EM) economies have taken more direct action. 
Many have already begun to raise policy interest rates over the past 
couple of months. Since the beginning of September, 23 EM central 
banks have raised their policy interest rate, some more than once. The 
most aggressive moves were a larger-than-expected 125-basis-point 
hike by Chile’s central bank on October 13, its third increase since the 
middle of July, and a 125-bp hike by Paraguay on October 21. The 
Reserve Bank of New Zealand joined the Norges Bank as the second 
DM central bank to raise it policy rate with a widely expected 25-bp 
increase on October 6. The RBNZ had surprisingly ended its asset 
purchase program in July and had signaled a rate hike for its August 
meeting. However, the surge in new Delta-variant cases prompted it to 
defer in August, but not in October. The Bank also signaled additional 
rate hikes ahead. More recently, the Bank of Canada immediately 
ended its asset purchase program at its October 27 meeting, citing 
stronger and more persistent-than-expected inflation and setting the 
stage for sooner-than-previously-expected policy rate hikes.
